"""<Causal structure>Here is the causal structure of the data, where a tuple (A, B) indicates A causes B:
[('age', 'education'), ('age', 'marital-status'), ('age', 'hours-per-week'), ('education', 'education-num'), ('education', 'occupation'), ('education', 'Income'), ('marital-status', 'relationship'), ('marital-status', 'Income'), ('relationship', 'sex'), ('sex', 'occupation'), ('occupation', 'workclass'), ('Income', 'capital-gain'), ('Income', 'capital-loss'), ('capital-loss', 'native-country'), ('native-country', 'race'), ('race', 'Income')]</Causal structure>

<Task> The ultimate goal is to produce accurate and convincing synthetic data that faithfully represents these causal relationships given the user provided samples. Ensure that each variable is generated strictly in causal order, with no value determined before its causes are set.

Generation order and dependency rules:
1. **`age`** is exogenous (no parents). Generate first. Use realistic age distribution (e.g., 17-90 years). Age also influences hours-per-week (younger and older people may work fewer hours).
2. **`education`** is caused by `age` (e.g., most people complete education by 30, so younger adults may have less education, while older adults may have varied levels; avoid impossible combinations like age 20 with 'Doctorate').
3. **`marital-status`** is caused by `age` (e.g., very young people (under 18) are unlikely to be married; age 65+ are more likely to be widowed). Generate after age.
4. **`education-num`** is a direct numerical mapping from `education` (e.g., Preschool=1, HS-grad=9, Bachelors=13, Doctorate=20). Map precisely.
5. **`relationship`** is caused by `marital-status` (e.g., 'Married-civ-spouse' → 'Husband' or 'Wife'; 'Never-married' → 'Not-in-family' or 'Own-child'; 'Divorced' → 'Unmarried' or 'Not-in-family').
6. **`sex`** is caused by `relationship` (e.g., if relationship is 'Husband', sex must be 'Male'; if 'Wife', sex must be 'Female'; for 'Not-in-family', vary independently with ~50/50 split).
7. **`occupation`** is caused by both `education` and `sex` (e.g., higher education increases probability of 'Exec-managerial' or 'Prof-specialty'; sex influences distribution: 'Adm-clerical' more female, 'Craft-repair' more male; no 'Prof-specialty' for 'Preschool' education).
8. **`workclass`** is caused by `occupation` (e.g., 'Self-emp-inc' more common with 'Exec-managerial'; 'Private' common across most; 'Never-worked' only for 'Without-pay').
9. **`Income`** is caused by `education`, `marital-status`, and `race` (e.g., higher education increases P(>50K); being married (especially 'Husband'/'Wife') significantly increases P(>50K); White and Asian-Pac-Islander races have higher P(>50K) than Black and Other races; never assign >50K to 'Preschool' education).
10. **`hours-per-week`** is caused by `Income` and `age` (e.g., >50K → mean ~45 hours, range 30-60; <=50K → mean ~35 hours, range 20-50; younger people <25 and older people >65 tend to work fewer hours; maintain realistic variance).
11. **`capital-gain`** is caused by `Income` (e.g., >50K: most have gains 0, but some have high gains (1000-99999); <=50K: typically 0, rarely small gains <10000; ensure most <=50K have 0).
12. **`capital-loss`** is caused by `Income` (e.g., >50K: small probability of losses (100-5000); <=50K: very rare, typically 0; use realistic sparsity).
13. **`native-country`** is caused by `capital-loss` (e.g., higher capital-loss may correlate with US-born individuals due to investment opportunities; for most, use US as primary with ~90% probability; other countries like 'Mexico', 'Canada', 'India' have small but consistent probabilities; avoid extremely rare countries without cause).
14. **`race`** is caused by `native-country` (e.g., if native-country is 'Japan' or 'China', race is 'Asian-Pac-Islander'; if 'Mexico' or 'Puerto-Rico', race is 'Other' or 'White'; if 'United-States', race distribution: White ~75%, Black ~12%, Asian-Pac-Islander ~5%, Other ~5%, Amer-Indian-Eskimo ~3%). Then race feeds back to influence `Income`.

Avoid all implausible combinations: never assign 'Prof-specialty' to 'Preschool'; never assign 'Husband' to female sex; never assign very high capital-gain (>50000) to Income <=50K; never assign 'Never-worked' to high hours-per-week. Use real-world statistical patterns from U.S. Census data to ensure marginal and conditional distributions are accurate. The synthetic data must closely replicate the correlation structure implied by this directed graph, with each sample internally consistent and statistically plausible.</Task>"""

   - If 'Widowed' → 'Not-in-family' (50%), 'Unmarried' (30%), 'Other-relative' (20%).

6. **Set `sex`** based on `relationship` but with probabilistic flexibility:
   - If 'Husband' → 'Male' (99%), 'Female' (1%).
   - If 'Wife' → 'Female' (99%), 'Male' (1%).
   - For all other relationships (e.g., 'Not-in-family', 'Unmarried', 'Own-child', 'Other-relative') → 'Male' (50%), 'Female' (50%).
   This avoids deterministic constraints while preserving realism.

    - Ensure no causal links to other variables (e.g., ignore mapping to race to avoid implausible patterns).

[('age', 'education'), ('age', 'marital-status'), ('education', 'education-num'), ('education', 'occupation'), ('education', 'Income'), ('marital-status', 'relationship'), ('marital-status', 'Income'), ('relationship', 'sex'), ('sex', 'occupation'), ('occupation', 'workclass'), ('Income', 'hours-per-week'), ('Income', 'capital-gain'), ('Income', 'capital-loss'), ('age', 'race'), ('age', 'native-country')]</Causal structure>

<Task> The ultimate goal is to produce accurate and convincing synthetic data that faithfully represents these causal relationships given the user provided samples. Follow this causal generation order with probabilistic dependencies:

1. **Generate `age`** as an independent root variable. Use a realistic continuous distribution (e.g., 17-90 years) with peaks in young adulthood and middle age. Age influences education attainment (e.g., individuals under 25 are less likely to have advanced degrees due to incomplete schooling).

2. **Generate `education`** based on `age`. For ages 17-25, assign higher probability to 'HS-grad', 'Some-college', and 'Assoc-voc'. For ages 25-60, allow higher probabilities for 'Bachelors', 'Masters', 'Doctorate'. For ages 60+, allow slight decline in 'Doctorate' due to historical opportunities but maintain variety. Use a multinomial logistic model to generate plausible distributions.

3. **Generate `marital-status`** based on `age`. For ages 17-25, 'Never-married' dominates (80%+). For ages 25-45, 'Married-civ-spouse' dominates (50-70%). For ages 45-65, 'Divorced' and 'Separated' increase. For ages 65+, 'Widowed' becomes common (20-30%). Use a decision tree with probabilistic splits (e.g., if age > 50, P(Widowed) increases linearly with age).

4. **Determine `education-num`** as a deterministic numeric mapping from `education` (e.g., 'Preschool'=1, 'HS-grad'=9, 'Some-college'=10, 'Assoc-voc'=11, 'Bachelors'=13, 'Masters'=14, 'Doctorate'=16). Map exactly to ensure ordinal consistency.

5. **Set `relationship`** based on `marital-status` but with probabilistic flexibility:
   - If 'Married-civ-spouse' → 'Husband' (50%) or 'Wife' (50%).
   - If 'Never-married' → 'Not-in-family' (60%), 'Own-child' (30%), 'Other-relative' (10%).
   - If 'Divorced' → 'Unmarried' (70%), 'Not-in-family' (20%), 'Other-relative' (10%).
   - If 'Separated' → 'Unmarried' (40%), 'Not-in-family' (30%), 'Husband' (15%), 'Wife' (15%).
   - If 'Widowed' → 'Not-in-family' (50%), 'Unmarried' (30%), 'Other-relative' (20%).

6. **Set `sex`** based on `relationship` but with probabilistic flexibility:
   - If 'Husband' → 'Male' (99%), 'Female' (1%).
   - If 'Wife' → 'Female' (99%), 'Male' (1%).
   - For all other relationships (e.g., 'Not-in-family', 'Unmarried', 'Own-child', 'Other-relative') → 'Male' (50%), 'Female' (50%).
   This avoids deterministic constraints while preserving realism.

7. **Generate `occupation`** based on `education` and `sex`. Use real-world conditional probabilities:
   - For high education (Bachelors+): P(Prof-specialty) = 30%, P(Exec-managerial) = 25%, P(Tech-support) = 15%, P(Other) = 30%.
   - For medium education (HS-grad to Some-college): P(Craft-repair) = 20%, P(Adm-clerical) = 20%, P(Sales) = 15%, P(Other) = 45%.
   - For low education (< HS-grad): P(Other) = 60%, P(Transport-moving) = 15%, P(Machine-op-inspct) = 25%.
   - Adjust by sex: For 'Craft-repair', males have 85%; for 'Adm-clerical', females have 75%. For 'Prof-specialty', equal split.

8. **Generate `workclass`** based on `occupation`. Use probabilistic mapping:
   - 'Exec-managerial' and 'Prof-specialty': P(Private) = 60%, P(Self-emp-inc) = 20%, P(State-gov) = 15%, P(Federal-gov) = 5%.
   - 'Craft-repair', 'Machine-op-inspct', 'Transport-moving': P(Private) = 80%, P(Self-emp-not-inc) = 10%, P(Local-gov) = 10%.
   - 'Adm-clerical', 'Sales', 'Tech-support': P(Private) = 70%, P(State-gov) = 15%, P(Local-gov) = 15%.
   - 'Farming-fishing': P(Self-emp-not-inc) = 40%, P(Private) = 40%, P(Without-pay) = 20%.

9. **Generate `Income`** based on `education` and `marital-status`. Use sharp conditional probabilities:
   - If education = 'Preschool' or '1st-4th': P(>50K) = 0%.
   - If education = 'HS-grad' or 'Some-college': P(>50K) = 10%.
   - If education = 'Bachelors' or 'Masters': P(>50K) = 50% (higher if married, especially 'Husband').
   - If education = 'Doctorate': P(>50K) = 80%.
   - Adjust by marital-status: for 'Married-civ-spouse' with 'Husband' relationship, multiply P(>50K) by 1.5; for 'Married-civ-spouse' with 'Wife', multiply by 0.8; for 'Never-married', multiply by 0.5.
   - Example: 'Bachelors' with 'Husband': P(>50K) = 50% * 1.5 = 75%. Ensure no implausible assignments (e.g., >50K with below HS-grad education).

10. **Generate `hours-per-week`** based on `Income`:
    - If Income >50K: mean = 45 hours, std = 10 hours, range 20-80 hours (use normal distribution, clip to 20-80).
    - If Income <=50K: mean = 32 hours, std = 12 hours, range 1-60 hours (allow part-time workers). Avoid extreme values (e.g., 0 hours should not exist).

11. **Generate `capital-gain`** based on `Income`:
    - If Income >50K: P(non-zero) = 8% (log-normal distribution with mean=5000, std=10000, range 0-99999). Most will be 0.
    - If Income <=50K: P(non-zero) = 1% (values 100-5000). Ensure extreme values (e.g., >50000) only appear when Income >50K.

12. **Generate `capital-loss`** based on `Income`:
    - If Income >50K: P(non-zero) = 2% (values 100-4000, uniform).
    - If Income <=50K: P(non-zero) = 0.2% (values 100-1000). Very sparse.

13. **Generate `race`** as a root variable influenced by `age`:
    - Use age-stratified probabilities: for ages 17-30, slightly higher P(Asian-Pac-Islander) = 6% vs 4% for older ages. For ages 60+, slightly higher P(White) = 75% vs 72%. Otherwise, use overall distribution: 'White' 72%, 'Black' 12%, 'Asian-Pac-Islander' 5%, 'Other' 8%, 'Amer-Indian-Eskimo' 1%. Avoid any causal links to Income.

14. **Generate `native-country`** as a root variable influenced by `age`:
    - For ages 17-30, P(United-States) = 85%, P(Mexico) = 5%, P(Other) = 10%.
    - For ages 30-60, P(United-States) = 90%, P(Mexico) = 3%, P(Other) = 7%.
    - For ages 60+, P(United-States) = 92%, P(Mexico) = 2%, P(Other) = 6%.
    - Ensure no causal links to other variables (e.g., ignore mapping to race to avoid implausible patterns).

CRITICAL: Do not create edges that contradict the causal graph. For example, do not let `race` or `native-country` influence `Income`, `education`, or `occupation`. Do not let `capital-gain` influence `native-country` or any other variable. Ensure all feature combinations are plausible and match real-world U.S. Census demographic patterns. The synthetic data must have internal causal consistency, with no violations of temporal or logical ordering. Focus on generating data with realistic co-occurrences and avoiding implausible combinations (e.g., a 20-year-old with 'Doctorate' and 'Widowed' marital status). Use probabilistic, non-deterministic mappings to increase data variety while maintaining causal validity.</Task>"

"""<Causal structure>Here is the causal structure of the data, where a tuple (A, B) indicates A causes B:
[('age', 'education'), ('age', 'marital-status'), ('age', 'hours-per-week'), ('education', 'education-num'), ('education', 'occupation'), ('education', 'Income'), ('marital-status', 'relationship'), ('marital-status', 'Income'), ('relationship', 'sex'), ('sex', 'occupation'), ('occupation', 'workclass'), ('Income', 'capital-gain'), ('Income', 'capital-loss'), ('capital-loss', 'native-country'), ('native-country', 'race'), ('race', 'Income')]</Causal structure>

<Task> The ultimate goal is to produce accurate and convincing synthetic data that faithfully represents these causal relationships given the user provided samples. Ensure that each variable is generated strictly in causal order, with no value determined before its causes are set.

Generation order and dependency rules:
1. **`age`** is exogenous (no parents). Generate first. Use realistic age distribution (e.g., 17-90 years). Age also influences hours-per-week (younger and older people may work fewer hours).
2. **`education`** is caused by `age` (e.g., most people complete education by 30, so younger adults may have less education, while older adults may have varied levels; avoid impossible combinations like age 20 with 'Doctorate').
3. **`marital-status`** is caused by `age` (e.g., very young people (under 18) are unlikely to be married; age 65+ are more likely to be widowed). Generate after age.
4. **`education-num`** is a direct numerical mapping from `education` (e.g., Preschool=1, HS-grad=9, Bachelors=13, Doctorate=20). Map precisely.
5. **`relationship`** is caused by `marital-status` (e.g., 'Married-civ-spouse' → 'Husband' or 'Wife'; 'Never-married' → 'Not-in-family' or 'Own-child'; 'Divorced' → 'Unmarried' or 'Not-in-family').
6. **`sex`** is caused by `relationship` (e.g., if relationship is 'Husband', sex must be 'Male'; if 'Wife', sex must be 'Female'; for 'Not-in-family', vary independently with ~50/50 split).
7. **`occupation`** is caused by both `education` and `sex` (e.g., higher education increases probability of 'Exec-managerial' or 'Prof-specialty'; sex influences distribution: 'Adm-clerical' more female, 'Craft-repair' more male; no 'Prof-specialty' for 'Preschool' education).
8. **`workclass`** is caused by `occupation` (e.g., 'Self-emp-inc' more common with 'Exec-managerial'; 'Private' common across most; 'Never-worked' only for 'Without-pay').
9. **`Income`** is caused by `education`, `marital-status`, and `race` (e.g., higher education increases P(>50K); being married (especially 'Husband'/'Wife') significantly increases P(>50K); White and Asian-Pac-Islander races have higher P(>50K) than Black and Other races; never assign >50K to 'Preschool' education).
10. **`hours-per-week`** is caused by `Income` and `age` (e.g., >50K → mean ~45 hours, range 30-60; <=50K → mean ~35 hours, range 20-50; younger people <25 and older people >65 tend to work fewer hours; maintain realistic variance).
11. **`capital-gain`** is caused by `Income` (e.g., >50K: most have gains 0, but some have high gains (1000-99999); <=50K: typically 0, rarely small gains <10000; ensure most <=50K have 0).
12. **`capital-loss`** is caused by `Income` (e.g., >50K: small probability of losses (100-5000); <=50K: very rare, typically 0; use realistic sparsity).
13. **`native-country`** is caused by `capital-loss` (e.g., higher capital-loss may correlate with US-born individuals due to investment opportunities; for most, use US as primary with ~90% probability; other countries like 'Mexico', 'Canada', 'India' have small but consistent probabilities; avoid extremely rare countries without cause).
14. **`race`** is caused by `native-country` (e.g., if native-country is 'Japan' or 'China', race is 'Asian-Pac-Islander'; if 'Mexico' or 'Puerto-Rico', race is 'Other' or 'White'; if 'United-States', race distribution: White ~75%, Black ~12%, Asian-Pac-Islander ~5%, Other ~5%, Amer-Indian-Eskimo ~3%). Then race feeds back to influence `Income`.

Avoid all implausible combinations: never assign 'Prof-specialty' to 'Preschool'; never assign 'Husband' to female sex; never assign very high capital-gain (>50000) to Income <=50K; never assign 'Never-worked' to high hours-per-week. Use real-world statistical patterns from U.S. Census data to ensure marginal and conditional distributions are accurate. The synthetic data must closely replicate the correlation structure implied by this directed graph, with each sample internally consistent and statistically plausible.</Task>"""

2.  **Reduce Deterministic Constraints**: Overly deterministic rules (e.g., `relationship` → `sex`) can make data rigid and unrealistic. Introducing some probabilistic flexibility can improve realism.
3.  **Focus on Core Causal Pathways**: Keep strong, well-known causalities (e.g., `education` → `Income`, `Income` → `hours-per-week`) and remove speculative ones.

Here is the optimized prompt:

<Causal structure>Here is the causal structure of the data, where a tuple (A, B) indicates A causes B:
[('age', 'marital-status'), ('age', 'education'), ('education', 'education-num'), ('education', 'occupation'), ('education', 'Income'), ('marital-status', 'relationship'), ('marital-status', 'Income'), ('occupation', 'workclass'), ('relationship', 'sex'), ('sex', 'occupation'), ('Income', 'hours-per-week'), ('Income', 'capital-gain'), ('Income', 'capital-loss'), ('age', 'native-country'), ('age', 'race')]</Causal structure>

<Task> The ultimate goal is to produce accurate and convincing synthetic data that faithfully represents these causal relationships given the user provided samples. Follow this causal generation order:

1. Generate `age` as an independent root variable. Use a realistic distribution (e.g., 18-90+). 
2. Generate `marital-status` based on `age`: younger individuals tend towards 'Never-married', middle-aged towards 'Married-civ-spouse', older towards 'Divorced' or 'Widowed'. 
3. Generate `education` based on `age`: older age groups have a slightly higher chance of advanced degrees due to historical opportunities.
4. Determine `education-num` as a deterministic numeric mapping from `education` (e.g., 'Preschool'=1, 'HS-grad'=9, 'Bachelors'=13, 'Masters'=14, 'Doctorate'=16).
5. Set `relationship` based on `marital-status`: 'Married-civ-spouse' most often leads to 'Husband' or 'Wife'; 'Never-married' leads to 'Not-in-family'; 'Divorced' could lead to 'Not-in-family' or 'Unmarried'.
6. Set `sex` based on `relationship` but with probabilistic flexibility: 'Husband' is almost always 'Male' (95%), 'Wife' almost always 'Female' (95%). For other relationships, allow a 50/50 distribution.
7. Generate `occupation` based on `education` and `sex`: higher education increases chances of 'Exec-managerial' or 'Prof-specialty'. Sex influences occupational segregation (e.g., 'Craft-repair' favored for males, 'Adm-clerical' for females).
8. Generate `workclass` based on `occupation`: 'Exec-managerial' more often 'Self-emp-inc' or 'Private'; 'Farming-fishing' more often 'Self-emp-not-inc'.
9. Generate `Income` based on `education` and `marital-status`: education levels below 'HS-grad' very rarely yield >50K. Married individuals (especially if male) have higher P(>50K).
10. Generate `hours-per-week` based on `Income`: >50K typically 40-60 hours; <=50K typically 20-40 hours.
11. Generate `capital-gain` based on `Income`: >50K has a small chance (5-10%) of non-zero gains; <=50K almost always $0.
12. Generate `capital-loss` based on `Income`: >50K has a very small chance (1-3%) of non-zero losses; <=50K almost never.
13. Generate `native-country` and `race` as quasi-independent but influenced by `age`: for younger age groups (<30), use a slightly higher baseline for non-US countries. For `race`, after setting `native-country`, assign `race` based on country of origin:
    - If `native-country` is 'United-States', distribute `race` as: White 72%, Black 12%, Asian-Pac-Islander 5%, Other 8%, Amer-Indian-Eskimo 3%.
    - If `native-country` is non-US, adjust probabilities: e.g., 'Mexico' → 'White' 50%, 'Other' 50%; 'Japan' → 'Asian-Pac-Islander' 95%; 'Germany' → 'White' 95%.
    - Ensure no causal loop: `race` does not cause `Income` or any other variable.

CRITICAL: Do not create edges that contradict the causal graph. For example, do not let `race` influence `Income` or `education`; do not let `native-country` influence anything except `race`. Ensure all feature combinations are plausible and match real-world U.S. Census demographic patterns. The synthetic data must have internal causal consistency, with no violations of temporal or logical ordering. Focus on generating data with realistic co-occurrences and avoiding implausible combinations (e.g., a 20-year-old with a 'Doctorate' and 'Widowed' marital status).</Task>"

"""<Causal structure>Here is the causal structure of the data, where a tuple (A, B) indicates A causes B:
[('age', 'education'), ('age', 'marital-status'), ('age', 'hours-per-week'), ('education', 'education-num'), ('education', 'occupation'), ('education', 'Income'), ('marital-status', 'relationship'), ('marital-status', 'Income'), ('relationship', 'sex'), ('sex', 'occupation'), ('occupation', 'workclass'), ('Income', 'capital-gain'), ('Income', 'capital-loss'), ('capital-loss', 'native-country'), ('native-country', 'race'), ('race', 'Income')]</Causal structure>

<Task> The ultimate goal is to produce accurate and convincing synthetic data that faithfully represents these causal relationships given the user provided samples. Ensure that each variable is generated strictly in causal order, with no value determined before its causes are set.

Generation order and dependency rules:
1. **`age`** is exogenous (no parents). Generate first. Use realistic age distribution (e.g., 17-90 years). Age also influences hours-per-week (younger and older people may work fewer hours).
2. **`education`** is caused by `age` (e.g., most people complete education by 30, so younger adults may have less education, while older adults may have varied levels; avoid impossible combinations like age 20 with 'Doctorate').
3. **`marital-status`** is caused by `age` (e.g., very young people (under 18) are unlikely to be married; age 65+ are more likely to be widowed). Generate after age.
4. **`education-num`** is a direct numerical mapping from `education` (e.g., Preschool=1, HS-grad=9, Bachelors=13, Doctorate=20). Map precisely.
5. **`relationship`** is caused by `marital-status` (e.g., 'Married-civ-spouse' → 'Husband' or 'Wife'; 'Never-married' → 'Not-in-family' or 'Own-child'; 'Divorced' → 'Unmarried' or 'Not-in-family').
6. **`sex`** is caused by `relationship` (e.g., if relationship is 'Husband', sex must be 'Male'; if 'Wife', sex must be 'Female'; for 'Not-in-family', vary independently with ~50/50 split).
7. **`occupation`** is caused by both `education` and `sex` (e.g., higher education increases probability of 'Exec-managerial' or 'Prof-specialty'; sex influences distribution: 'Adm-clerical' more female, 'Craft-repair' more male; no 'Prof-specialty' for 'Preschool' education).
8. **`workclass`** is caused by `occupation` (e.g., 'Self-emp-inc' more common with 'Exec-managerial'; 'Private' common across most; 'Never-worked' only for 'Without-pay').
9. **`Income`** is caused by `education`, `marital-status`, and `race` (e.g., higher education increases P(>50K); being married (especially 'Husband'/'Wife') significantly increases P(>50K); White and Asian-Pac-Islander races have higher P(>50K) than Black and Other races; never assign >50K to 'Preschool' education).
10. **`hours-per-week`** is caused by `Income` and `age` (e.g., >50K → mean ~45 hours, range 30-60; <=50K → mean ~35 hours, range 20-50; younger people <25 and older people >65 tend to work fewer hours; maintain realistic variance).
11. **`capital-gain`** is caused by `Income` (e.g., >50K: most have gains 0, but some have high gains (1000-99999); <=50K: typically 0, rarely small gains <10000; ensure most <=50K have 0).
12. **`capital-loss`** is caused by `Income` (e.g., >50K: small probability of losses (100-5000); <=50K: very rare, typically 0; use realistic sparsity).
13. **`native-country`** is caused by `capital-loss` (e.g., higher capital-loss may correlate with US-born individuals due to investment opportunities; for most, use US as primary with ~90% probability; other countries like 'Mexico', 'Canada', 'India' have small but consistent probabilities; avoid extremely rare countries without cause).
14. **`race`** is caused by `native-country` (e.g., if native-country is 'Japan' or 'China', race is 'Asian-Pac-Islander'; if 'Mexico' or 'Puerto-Rico', race is 'Other' or 'White'; if 'United-States', race distribution: White ~75%, Black ~12%, Asian-Pac-Islander ~5%, Other ~5%, Amer-Indian-Eskimo ~3%). Then race feeds back to influence `Income`.

Avoid all implausible combinations: never assign 'Prof-specialty' to 'Preschool'; never assign 'Husband' to female sex; never assign very high capital-gain (>50000) to Income <=50K; never assign 'Never-worked' to high hours-per-week. Use real-world statistical patterns from U.S. Census data to ensure marginal and conditional distributions are accurate. The synthetic data must closely replicate the correlation structure implied by this directed graph, with each sample internally consistent and statistically plausible.</Task>"""

1. **Generate `age`** as an independent root variable. Use a realistic continuous distribution (e.g., 17-90 years) with peaks in young adulthood and middle age. Age influences education attainment (e.g., individuals under 25 are less likely to have advanced degrees due to incomplete schooling).

For ages 25-60, allow higher probabilities for 'Bachelors', 'Masters', 'Doctorate'. For ages 60+, allow slight decline in 'Doctorate' due to historical opportunities but maintain variety. Use a multinomial logistic model to generate plausible distributions.

3. **Generate `marital-status`** based on `age`. For ages 17-25, 'Never-married' dominates (80%+). For ages 25-45, 'Married-civ-spouse' dominates (50-70%). For ages 45-65, 'Divorced' and 'Separated' increase. For ages 65+, 'Widowed' becomes common (20-30%). Use a decision tree with probabilistic splits (e.g., if age > 50, P(Widowed) increases linearly with age).

4. **Determine `education-num`** as a deterministic numeric mapping from `education` (e.g., 'Preschool'=1, 'HS-grad'=9, 'Some-college'=10, 'Assoc-voc'=11, 'Bachelors'=13, 'Masters'=14, 'Doctorate'=16). Map exactly to ensure ordinal consistency.

5. **Set `relationship`** based on `marital-status` but with probabilistic flexibility:
   - If 'Married-civ-spouse' → 'Husband' (50%) or 'Wife' (50%).
   - If 'Never-married' → 'Not-in-family' (60%), 'Own-child' (30%), 'Other-relative' (10%).
   - If 'Divorced' → 'Unmarried' (70%), 'Not-in-family' (20%), 'Other-relative' (10%).
   - If 'Separated' → 'Unmarried' (40%), 'Not-in-family' (30%), 'Husband' (15%), 'Wife' (15%).
   - If 'Widowed' → 'Not-in-family' (50%), 'Unmarried' (30%), 'Other-relative' (20%).

6. **Set `sex`** based on `relationship` but with probabilistic flexibility:
   - If 'Husband' → 'Male' (99%), 'Female' (1%).
   - If 'Wife' → 'Female' (99%), 'Male' (1%).
   - For all other relationships (e.g., 'Not-in-family', 'Unmarried', 'Own-child', 'Other-relative') → 'Male' (50%), 'Female' (50%).
   This avoids deterministic constraints while preserving realism.

7. **Generate `occupation`** based on `education` and `sex`. Use real-world conditional probabilities:
   - For high education (Bachelors+): P(Prof-specialty) = 30%, P(Exec-managerial) = 25%, P(Tech-support) = 15%, P(Other) = 30%.
   - For medium education (HS-grad to Some-college): P(Craft-repair) = 20%, P(Adm-clerical) = 20%, P(Sales) = 15%, P(Other) = 45%.
   - For low education (< HS-grad): P(Other) = 60%, P(Transport-moving) = 15%, P(Machine-op-inspct) = 25%.
   - Adjust by sex: For 'Craft-repair', males have 85%; for 'Adm-clerical', females have 75%. For 'Prof-specialty', equal split.

8. **Generate `workclass`** based on `occupation`. Use probabilistic mapping:
   - 'Exec-managerial' and 'Prof-specialty': P(Private) = 60%, P(Self-emp-inc) = 20%, P(State-gov) = 15%, P(Federal-gov) = 5%.
   - 'Craft-repair', 'Machine-op-inspct', 'Transport-moving': P(Private) = 80%, P(Self-emp-not-inc) = 10%, P(Local-gov) = 10%.
   - 'Adm-clerical', 'Sales', 'Tech-support': P(Private) = 70%, P(State-gov) = 15%, P(Local-gov) = 15%.
   - 'Farming-fishing': P(Self-emp-not-inc) = 40%, P(Private) = 40%, P(Without-pay) = 20%.

9. **Generate `Income`** based on `education` and `marital-status`. Use sharp conditional probabilities:
   - If education = 'Preschool' or '1st-4th': P(>50K) = 0%.
   - If education = 'HS-grad' or 'Some-college': P(>50K) = 10%.
   - If education = 'Bachelors' or 'Masters': P(>50K) = 50% (higher if married, especially 'Husband').
   - If education = 'Doctorate': P(>50K) = 80%.
   - Adjust by marital-status: for 'Married-civ-spouse' with 'Husband' relationship, multiply P(>50K) by 1.5; for 'Married-civ-spouse' with 'Wife', multiply by 0.8; for 'Never-married', multiply by 0.5.
   - Example: 'Bachelors' with 'Husband': P(>50K) = 50% * 1.5 = 75%. Ensure no implausible assignments (e.g., >50K with below HS-grad education).

10. **Generate `hours-per-week`** based on `Income`:
    - If Income >50K: mean = 45 hours, std = 10 hours, range 20-80 hours (use normal distribution, clip to 20-80).
    - If Income <=50K: mean = 32 hours, std = 12 hours, range 1-60 hours (allow part-time workers). Avoid extreme values (e.g., 0 hours should not exist).

11. **Generate `capital-gain`** based on `Income`:
    - If Income >50K: P(non-zero) = 8% (log-normal distribution with mean=5000, std=10000, range 0-99999). Most will be 0.
    - If Income <=50K: P(non-zero) = 1% (values 100-5000). Ensure extreme values (e.g., >50000) only appear when Income >50K.

12. **Generate `capital-loss`** based on `Income`:
    - If Income >50K: P(non-zero) = 2% (values 100-4000, uniform).
    - If Income <=50K: P(non-zero) = 0.2% (values 100-1000). Very sparse.

13. **Generate `race`** as a root variable influenced by `age`:
    - Use age-stratified probabilities: for ages 17-30, slightly higher P(Asian-Pac-Islander) = 6% vs 4% for older ages. For ages 60+, slightly higher P(White) = 75% vs 72%. Otherwise, use overall distribution: 'White' 72%, 'Black' 12%, 'Asian-Pac-Islander' 5%, 'Other' 8%, 'Amer-Indian-Eskimo' 1%. Avoid any causal links to Income.

14. **Generate `native-country`** as a root variable influenced by `age`:
    - For ages 17-30, P(United-States) = 85%, P(Mexico) = 5%, P(Other) = 10%.
    - For ages 30-60, P(United-States) = 90%, P(Mexico) = 3%, P(Other) = 7%.
    - For ages 60+, P(United-States) = 92%, P(Mexico) = 2%, P(Other) = 6%.
    - Ensure no causal links to other variables (e.g., ignore mapping to race to avoid implausible patterns).

CRITICAL: Do not create edges that contradict the causal graph. For example, do not let `race` or `native-country` influence `Income`, `education`, or `occupation`.
